Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 62 / industrial_truck_or_tractor_operator / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I'm here because the routing system at the warehouse isn't just a computer to me anymore—I know it has been marking my moves and sending messages through the scanners to push me out because I'm one of the old hands.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess I'm here because... it's the warehouse. The routing system down there, it isn't just a computer to me anymore. I know it has been marking my moves. I drive the industrial tractors, you know? Have for a long, long time. Back when I got my associate's degree, I thought I'd be doing something else, maybe drafting or something in an office. My buddy Jim did drafting. He moved out to Arizona a few years ago. Says the dry air is better for his joints. My knees have been killing me lately, honestly. Getting up into the cab of the truck is a real chore these days. 

But anyway, the system... it's sending messages through the scanners. I can see it happening. It's trying to push me out because I'm one of the old hands. They don't want us older guys around anymore. Jim retired early, maybe I should have too. But they just keep scanning, and the beeps... they sound different now, kind of like the dashboard light on my old Chevy when the transmission starts slipping. I really need to get that truck looked at, it's been stalling out at red lights...

I drive the industrial tractors, you know? Have for a long, long time. Back when I got my associate's degree, I thought I'd be doing something else, maybe drafting or something in an office. My buddy Jim did drafting. He moved out to Arizona a few years ago. Says the dry air is better for his joints. My knees have been killing me lately, honestly. One of them, I think his name was Kyle or maybe Kevin, he wore these bright red sneakers on the warehouse floor. Can you believe that? Red sneakers. No steel toes, no nothing. OSHA would have a field day with that. I remember when safety actually meant something. Back in '92, we had a guy drop a pallet of engine parts on his foot because he wasn't paying attention. Broke three toes. He was out for six weeks, and when he came back, he had this special boot he had to wear. It looked like a ski boot. I used to ski, actually, up in Vermont when I was younger. My brother had a cabin up there. We'd go up for the weekend, freeze our tails off, but it was beautiful. The snow up there is different, you know? It's like powder. Not like the slush we get down here. But yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started acting up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear the voices when you are away from the warehouse and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I've been trying to organize my tools, but it's tough. I bought this pegboard from the hardware store, the one over on 4th Street. Used to be a family-owned place, but a big chain bought them out last year. You can't find anyone to help you in there anymore. I walked up and down the aisles for twenty minutes just looking for some specific masonry drill bits. I was going to hang a shelf for my wife's gardening stuff. No, my memory is fine. I haven't noticed any issues with my thinking or anything like that. I mean, I still remember all the routing codes for the warehouse, even the old ones they phased out back in 2015. They switched to this new alphanumeric system, supposedly to make things more efficient, but it just confused half the guys on the floor. I remember when we used to just use a simple color-coded tag system. Red for outbound, blue for receiving, yellow for hazardous materials. Simple, right? You could look across the dock and know exactly what was where. Now you gotta scan a barcode that's half torn off, and the scanner beeps at you like you did something wrong. Those scanners, they run on these lithium-ion batteries that barely hold a charge for a full shift. I read an article in Popular Mechanics about how those batteries are terrible for the environment. They mine the stuff over in South America, I think. Huge open-pit mines. It's a shame what we're doing to the planet just so we can have our gadgets. I try to do my part, you know? I recycle all my aluminum cans. Been doing it for years. I crush them down with this heavy-duty can crusher I mounted on the wall in the garage right next to the pegboard I was telling you about... but yeah, my memory is sharp as a tack. No problems there.

The voices and that whole thing with the scanners, I'd say it's been about a year and eight months. Right around the time they brought in those new supervisor kids. You know, they hire these kids straight out of college, business degrees and all that, and they think they know everything about moving freight. One of them, I think his name was Kyle or maybe Kevin, he wore these bright red sneakers on the warehouse floor. Can you believe that? Red sneakers. No steel toes, no nothing. OSHA would have a field day with that. I remember when safety actually meant something. Back in '92, we had a guy drop a pallet of engine parts on his foot because he wasn't paying attention. Broke three toes. He was out for six weeks, and when he came back, he had this special boot he had to wear. It looked like a ski boot. I used to ski, actually, up in Vermont when I was younger. My brother had a cabin up there. We'd go up for the weekend, freeze our tails off, but it was beautiful. The snow up there is different, you know? It's like powder. Not like the slush we get down here. But yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started acting up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear the voices when you are away from the warehouse and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't stop when I clock out. Even when I'm just sitting out in my garage on a Saturday, you know, with the radio off, everything shut down... I hear the dispatch voices. Clear as a bell. They call out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous or that security is on their way to get me. I spend a lot of time in the garage these days. I've been trying to organize my tools, but it's tough. I bought this pegboard from the hardware store, the one over on 4th Street. Used to be a family-owned place, but a big chain bought them out last year. You can't find anyone to help you in there anymore. I walked up and down the aisles for twenty minutes just looking for some specific masonry drill bits. I was going to hang a shelf for my wife's gardening stuff. She loves her tomatoes, gets those heirloom seeds from a catalog... but yeah, the voices, they find me even in the garage.\n\nDoctor: Did anything medically significant happen around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't think of anything medical happening back then. I mean, my health's been about the same as it always is. Just the usual aches and pains, you know? Like I said about my knees. And my lower back, that acts up if I sit in the cab too long without stretching. I used to have this chiropractor, Dr. Evans. Great guy, had these strong hands, could crack your back and make you feel like a new man. He had this office down by the old strip mall, the one they tore down to build that new luxury apartment complex. Can you believe the rent they're charging for those places? Three thousand a month for a one-bedroom. Who can afford that? Mostly those tech kids, I guess. The ones who work from home and get their groceries delivered. I still like going to the store myself. You gotta pick your own produce, you know? You can't trust someone else to pick a good melon. You gotta thump it, smell the rind. My grandfather taught me that. He had a farm out in Ohio... but no, no big medical stuff around the time the scanners started acting up. Just the usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new problems with your memory or thinking since the voices and scanner messages began?\n\nPatient: No, my memory is fine. I haven't noticed any issues with my thinking or anything like that. I mean, I still remember all the routing codes for the warehouse, even the old ones they phased out back in 2015. They switched to this new alphanumeric system, supposedly to make things more efficient, but it just confused half the guys on the floor. I remember when we used to just use a simple color-coded tag system. Red for outbound, blue for receiving, yellow for hazardous materials. Simple, right? You could look across the dock and know exactly what was where. Now you gotta scan a barcode that's half torn off, and the scanner beeps at you like you did something wrong. Those scanners, they run on these lithium-ion batteries that barely hold a charge for a full shift. I read an article in Popular Mechanics about how those batteries are terrible for the environment. They mine the stuff over in South America, I think. Huge open-pit mines. It's a shame what we're doing to the planet just so we can have our gadgets. I try to do my part, you know? I recycle all my aluminum cans. Been doing it for years. I crush them down with this heavy-duty can crusher I mounted on the wall in the garage right next to the pegboard I was telling you about... but yeah, my memory is sharp as a tack. No problems there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, after that whole incident with the forklift, they actually took me off operating duties. Put me on leave. They told me I wasn't safe around the equipment anymore. Which is ridiculous, I've been driving those things for thirty years. I know every inch of a Crown reach truck. Did you know they manufacture those right here in the US? Over in Ohio, I think. Same place my grandfather had his farm. Anyway, they said I was a liability. So now I'm just at home. And honestly, my wife has had to take over basically everything. The bills, making appointments, most of the chores around the house. I just can't seem to keep up with it all reliably anymore. She's got this whole system now with a binder and color-coded tabs for the bills. Reminds me of the old color-coded tags we used to have at the warehouse before they brought in those scanners. She uses those little sticky flags, the ones that you can write on? I tried to buy her some more at the office supply store, but they were completely out of the neon green ones. She only likes the neon green for the electric bill. I don't know why, maybe because electricity is energy and green means go? Or maybe it's just her favorite color. Her sister's favorite color is purple, and she drives this awful purple PT Cruiser. Have you ever seen one of those? They look like little hearses. I don't know what Chrysler was thinking when they designed that car. It's got no horsepower, either. But yeah, I'm on leave and she's doing all the heavy lifting at home.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's not just the scanner screens, if that's what you mean. The voices... they don't stop when I leave the warehouse. I can be sitting right there in my garage, you know, out by the pegboard where I keep all my tools. I like to keep everything organized by size. Wrenches on the left, screwdrivers on the right. Phillips head, flathead, Torx... you really need a Torx set these days if you want to fix anything electronic. They use those special star-shaped screws just to keep you from opening things up and fixing them yourself. It's a racket, really. Planned obsolescence, they call it. They want you to just throw it away and buy a new one. Like those toaster ovens. I had one from the 80s that lasted twenty years. The new one I bought? The heating element went out in six months. Six months! And when I tried to take the back off, sure enough, Torx screws. But anyway, I'll be sitting out there in the garage, everything shut off, quiet as can be, and I'll hear the dispatch voices. Clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous. Saying security is coming for me. It sounds just like the PA system at work, but I'm miles away. My wife says it's just the wind or the neighbor's TV, but I know what I hear. Our neighbor, Frank, he does watch his TV way too loud. He's hard of hearing, refuses to get hearing aids. Says they cost too much. Which they do, honestly. The markup on medical devices is criminal. But Frank's TV doesn't sound like dispatch. Dispatch has this metallic echo to it. And they're definitely talking to me.

Not like the slush we get down here. They call out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous or that security is on their way to get me. I spend a lot of time in the garage these days. I've been trying to organize my tools, but it's tough. I bought this pegboard from the hardware store, the one over on 4th Street. Used to be a family-owned place, but a big chain bought them out last year. You can't find anyone to help you in there anymore. I walked up and down the aisles for twenty minutes just looking for some specific masonry drill bits. I was going to hang a shelf for my wife's gardening stuff. She loves her tomatoes, gets those heirloom seeds from a catalog... but yeah, the voices, they find me even in the garage.\n\nDoctor: Did anything medically significant happen around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't think of anything medical happening back then. I mean, my health's been about the same as it always is. Just the usual aches and pains, you know? Like I said about my knees. And my lower back, that acts up if I sit in the cab too long without stretching. I used to have this chiropractor, Dr. Evans. Great guy, had these strong hands, could crack your back and make you feel like a new man. He had this office down by the old strip mall, the one they tore down to build that new luxury apartment complex. Can you believe the rent they're charging for those places? Three thousand a month for a one-bedroom. Who can afford that? Mostly those tech kids, I guess. The ones who work from home and get their groceries delivered. I still like going to the store myself. You gotta pick your own produce, you know? You can't trust someone else to pick a good melon. You gotta thump it, smell the rind. My grandfather taught me that. He had a farm out in Ohio... but no, no big medical stuff around the time the scanners started acting up. Just the usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new problems with your memory or thinking since the voices and scanner messages began?\n\nPatient: No, my memory is fine. I haven't noticed any issues with my thinking or anything like that. I mean, I still remember all the routing codes for the warehouse, even the old ones they phased out back in 2015. They switched to this new alphanumeric system, supposedly to make things more efficient, but it just confused half the guys on the floor. I remember when we used to just use a simple color-coded tag system. Red for outbound, blue for receiving, yellow for hazardous materials. Simple, right? You could look across the dock and know exactly what was where. Now you gotta scan a barcode that's half torn off, and the scanner beeps at you like you did something wrong. Those scanners, they run on these lithium-ion batteries that barely hold a charge for a full shift. I read an article in Popular Mechanics about how those batteries are terrible for the environment. They mine the stuff over in South America, I think. Huge open-pit mines. It's a shame what we're doing to the planet just so we can have our gadgets. I try to do my part, you know? I recycle all my aluminum cans. Been doing it for years. I crush them down with this heavy-duty can crusher I mounted on the wall in the garage right next to the pegboard I was telling you about... but yeah, my memory is sharp as a tack. No problems there.\n\nDoctor: Since the voices and scanner messages began, how has your ability to do your job changed?\n\nPatient: Well, after that whole incident with the forklift, they actually took me off operating duties. Put me on leave. They told me I wasn't safe around the equipment anymore. Which is ridiculous, I've been driving those things for thirty years. I know every inch of a Crown reach truck. Did you know they manufacture those right here in the US? Over in Ohio, I think. Same place my grandfather had his farm. Anyway, they said I was a liability. So now I'm just at home. And honestly, my wife has had to take over basically everything. The bills, making appointments, most of the chores around the house. I just can't seem to keep up with it all reliably anymore. She's got this whole system now with a binder and color-coded tabs for the bills. Reminds me of the old color-coded tags we used to have at the warehouse before they brought in those scanners. She uses those little sticky flags, the ones that you can write on? I tried to buy her some more at the office supply store, but they were completely out of the neon green ones. Phillips head, flathead, Torx... you really need a Torx set these days if you want to fix anything electronic. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah... I guess I have. I used to love going fishing. Every Sunday morning, out at the lake before the sun even came up. The water is so still then, you know? It's like glass. And my choir practice, too. I've been singing bass in the church choir for fifteen years. We did this beautiful rendition of 'How Great Thou Art' last Easter. The acoustics in our church are fantastic, they built it back in the 50s with real oak paneling. Not like the cheap drywall they use everywhere now. You can really hear the resonance when the whole choir hits a chord. But lately... I don't know. I just don't go anymore. Same with my garage. I used to spend hours out there, tinkering with wood, building birdhouses, fixing things. I got this beautiful set of chisels for my birthday a few years ago. High-carbon steel, made in Sheffield, England. They hold an edge like you wouldn't believe. You have to sharpen them with a wet stone, though, none of that electric grinder nonsense or you'll ruin the temper of the metal. I keep them right there on the pegboard. But now... I can go out to the garage, sit there, look right at my rods, look at my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them. No desire to make anything. I just sit there. And then I hear the dispatch voices, like I said. So yeah, I guess I don't care about those things much right now.

I don't know what Chrysler was thinking when they designed that car. It's got no horsepower, either. Phillips head, flathead, Torx... you really need a Torx set these days if you want to fix anything electronic. They want you to just throw it away and buy a new one. Like those toaster ovens. I had one from the 80s that lasted twenty years. The new one I bought? The heating element went out in six months. Six months! And when I tried to take the back off, sure enough, Torx screws. Says they cost too much. Which they do, honestly. The markup on medical devices is criminal. But Frank's TV doesn't sound like dispatch. Dispatch has this metallic echo to it. And they're definitely talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you lost interest in activities or relationships that used to matter to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... I've been singing bass in the church choir for fifteen years. We did this beautiful rendition of 'How Great Thou Art' last Easter. The acoustics in our church are fantastic, they built it back in the 50s with real oak paneling. Not like the cheap drywall they use everywhere now. You can really hear the resonance when the whole choir hits a chord. But lately... I don't know. I just don't go anymore. Same with my garage. I used to spend hours out there, tinkering with wood, building birdhouses, fixing things. I got this beautiful set of chisels for my birthday a few years ago. High-carbon steel, made in Sheffield, England. They hold an edge like you wouldn't believe. You have to sharpen them with a wet stone, though, none of that electric grinder nonsense or you'll ruin the temper of the metal. I keep them right there on the pegboard. But now... I can go out to the garage, sit there, look right at my rods, look at my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them. No desire to make anything. I just sit there. And then I hear the dispatch voices, like I said. So yeah, I guess I don't care about those things much right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, they do. Actually, there was a stretch a little while back... my mood had eased up some, you know? I wasn't feeling so down in the dumps. I was even eating a bit more, talking a little more with my wife. We had meatloaf one night. She makes a really good meatloaf, puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It caramelizes in the oven, gives it this nice crust. You have to use a mix of beef and pork, though, or it gets too dry. Some people use veal, but I think veal is just too expensive these days. Have you seen the price of meat at the grocery store? It's highway robbery. Ground chuck is up to five dollars a pound. I remember when it was ninety-nine cents. You could feed a whole family on five bucks back then. But anyway, even during those weeks when I was feeling a bit better, eating the meatloaf and all, I still heard dispatch. Clear as a bell. They were still calling out my operator number, and I still knew that the routing system was tracking my movements. The mood was better, but the tracking and the voices... they didn't stop. Not at all. It makes you wonder how the system works, you know? If it's satellite-based or if it's using the local cell towers. They say 5G can pinpoint you within a few feet. I read an article about that in Popular Mechanics. I've had a subscription to Popular Mechanics since 1985. Great magazine.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F29\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because... it's the warehouse. The routing system down there, it isn't just a computer to me anymore. I know it has been marking my moves. I drive the industrial tractors, you know? Have for a long, long time. Back when I got my associate's degree, I thought I'd be doing something else, maybe drafting or something in an office. My buddy Jim did drafting. He moved out to Arizona a few years ago. Says the dry air is better for his joints. My knees have been killing me lately, honestly. Getting up into the cab of the truck is a real chore these days. \n\nBut anyway, the system... it's sending messages through the scanners. I can see it happening. It's trying to push me out because I'm one of the old hands. They don't want us older guys around anymore. Jim retired early, maybe I should have too. But they just keep scanning, and the beeps... they sound different now, kind of like the dashboard light on my old Chevy when the transmission starts slipping. I really need to get that truck looked at, it's been stalling out at red lights...\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed that the warehouse routing system is sending messages specifically to you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on for a while now. The voices and that whole thing with the scanners, I'd say it's been about a year and eight months. Right around the time they brought in those new supervisor kids. You know, they hire these kids straight out of college, business degrees and all that, and they think they know everything about moving freight. One of them, I think his name was Kyle or maybe Kevin, he wore these bright red sneakers on the warehouse floor. Can you believe that? Red sneakers. No steel toes, no nothing. OSHA would have a field day with that. I remember when safety actually meant something. Back in '92, we had a guy drop a pallet of engine parts on his foot because he wasn't paying attention. Broke three toes. He was out for six weeks, and when he came back, he had this special boot he had to wear. It looked like a ski boot. I used to ski, actually, up in Vermont when I was younger. My brother had a cabin up there. We'd go up for the weekend, freeze our tails off, but it was beautiful. The snow up there is different, you know? It's like powder. Not like the slush we get down here. But yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started acting up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear the voices when you are away from the warehouse and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't stop when I clock out. Even when I'm just sitting out in my garage on a Saturday, you know, with the radio off, everything shut down... I hear the dispatch voices. Clear as a bell. They call out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous or that security is on their way to get me. I spend a lot of time in the garage these days. I've been trying to organize my tools, but it's tough. I bought this pegboard from the hardware store, the one over on 4th Street. Used to be a family-owned place, but a big chain bought them out last year. You can't find anyone to help you in there anymore. I walked up and down the aisles for twenty minutes just looking for some specific masonry drill bits. I was going to hang a shelf for my wife's gardening stuff. She loves her tomatoes, gets those heirloom seeds from a catalog... but yeah, the voices, they find me even in the garage.\n\nDoctor: Did anything medically significant happen around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't think of anything medical happening back then. I mean, my health's been about the same as it always is. Just the usual aches and pains, you know? Like I said about my knees. And my lower back, that acts up if I sit in the cab too long without stretching. I used to have this chiropractor, Dr. Evans. Great guy, had these strong hands, could crack your back and make you feel like a new man. He had this office down by the old strip mall, the one they tore down to build that new luxury apartment complex. Can you believe the rent they're charging for those places? Three thousand a month for a one-bedroom. Who can afford that? Mostly those tech kids, I guess. The ones who work from home and get their groceries delivered. I still like going to the store myself. You gotta pick your own produce, you know? You can't trust someone else to pick a good melon. You gotta thump it, smell the rind. My grandfather taught me that. He had a farm out in Ohio... but no, no big medical stuff around the time the scanners started acting up. Just the usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new problems with your memory or thinking since the voices and scanner messages began?\n\nPatient: No, my memory is fine. I haven't noticed any issues with my thinking or anything like that. I mean, I still remember all the routing codes for the warehouse, even the old ones they phased out back in 2015. They switched to this new alphanumeric system, supposedly to make things more efficient, but it just confused half the guys on the floor. I remember when we used to just use a simple color-coded tag system. Red for outbound, blue for receiving, yellow for hazardous materials. Simple, right? You could look across the dock and know exactly what was where. Now you gotta scan a barcode that's half torn off, and the scanner beeps at you like you did something wrong. Those scanners, they run on these lithium-ion batteries that barely hold a charge for a full shift. I read an article in Popular Mechanics about how those batteries are terrible for the environment. They mine the stuff over in South America, I think. Huge open-pit mines. It's a shame what we're doing to the planet just so we can have our gadgets. I try to do my part, you know? I recycle all my aluminum cans. Been doing it for years. I crush them down with this heavy-duty can crusher I mounted on the wall in the garage right next to the pegboard I was telling you about... but yeah, my memory is sharp as a tack. No problems there.\n\nDoctor: Since the voices and scanner messages began, how has your ability to do your job changed?\n\nPatient: Well, after that whole incident with the forklift, they actually took me off operating duties. Put me on leave. They told me I wasn't safe around the equipment anymore. Which is ridiculous, I've been driving those things for thirty years. I know every inch of a Crown reach truck. Did you know they manufacture those right here in the US? Over in Ohio, I think. Same place my grandfather had his farm. Anyway, they said I was a liability. So now I'm just at home. And honestly, my wife has had to take over basically everything. The bills, making appointments, most of the chores around the house. I just can't seem to keep up with it all reliably anymore. She's got this whole system now with a binder and color-coded tabs for the bills. Reminds me of the old color-coded tags we used to have at the warehouse before they brought in those scanners. She uses those little sticky flags, the ones that you can write on? I tried to buy her some more at the office supply store, but they were completely out of the neon green ones. She only likes the neon green for the electric bill. I don't know why, maybe because electricity is energy and green means go? Or maybe it's just her favorite color. Her sister's favorite color is purple, and she drives this awful purple PT Cruiser. Have you ever seen one of those? They look like little hearses. I don't know what Chrysler was thinking when they designed that car. It's got no horsepower, either. But yeah, I'm on leave and she's doing all the heavy lifting at home.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the warehouse system and its messages, have you had any other unusual experiences or beliefs that people around you do not share?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just the scanner screens, if that's what you mean. The voices... they don't stop when I leave the warehouse. I can be sitting right there in my garage, you know, out by the pegboard where I keep all my tools. I like to keep everything organized by size. Wrenches on the left, screwdrivers on the right. Phillips head, flathead, Torx... you really need a Torx set these days if you want to fix anything electronic. They use those special star-shaped screws just to keep you from opening things up and fixing them yourself. It's a racket, really. Planned obsolescence, they call it. They want you to just throw it away and buy a new one. Like those toaster ovens. I had one from the 80s that lasted twenty years. The new one I bought? The heating element went out in six months. Six months! And when I tried to take the back off, sure enough, Torx screws. But anyway, I'll be sitting out there in the garage, everything shut off, quiet as can be, and I'll hear the dispatch voices. Clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous. Saying security is coming for me. It sounds just like the PA system at work, but I'm miles away. My wife says it's just the wind or the neighbor's TV, but I know what I hear. Our neighbor, Frank, he does watch his TV way too loud. He's hard of hearing, refuses to get hearing aids. Says they cost too much. Which they do, honestly. The markup on medical devices is criminal. But Frank's TV doesn't sound like dispatch. Dispatch has this metallic echo to it. And they're definitely talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you lost interest in activities or relationships that used to matter to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... I guess I have. I used to love going fishing. Every Sunday morning, out at the lake before the sun even came up. The water is so still then, you know? It's like glass. And my choir practice, too. I've been singing bass in the church choir for fifteen years. We did this beautiful rendition of 'How Great Thou Art' last Easter. The acoustics in our church are fantastic, they built it back in the 50s with real oak paneling. Not like the cheap drywall they use everywhere now. You can really hear the resonance when the whole choir hits a chord. But lately... I don't know. I just don't go anymore. Same with my garage. I used to spend hours out there, tinkering with wood, building birdhouses, fixing things. I got this beautiful set of chisels for my birthday a few years ago. High-carbon steel, made in Sheffield, England. They hold an edge like you wouldn't believe. You have to sharpen them with a wet stone, though, none of that electric grinder nonsense or you'll ruin the temper of the metal. I keep them right there on the pegboard. But now... I can go out to the garage, sit there, look right at my rods, look at my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them. No desire to make anything. I just sit there. And then I hear the dispatch voices, like I said. So yeah, I guess I don't care about those things much right now.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices and beliefs continued during periods when you were not feeling depressed or unusually energized?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, they do. Actually, there was a stretch a little while back... my mood had eased up some, you know? I wasn't feeling so down in the dumps. I was even eating a bit more, talking a little more with my wife. We had meatloaf one night. She makes a really good meatloaf, puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It caramelizes in the oven, gives it this nice crust. You have to use a mix of beef and pork, though, or it gets too dry. Some people use veal, but I think veal is just too expensive these days. Have you seen the price of meat at the grocery store? It's highway robbery. Ground chuck is up to five dollars a pound. I remember when it was ninety-nine cents. You could feed a whole family on five bucks back then. But anyway, even during those weeks when I was feeling a bit better, eating the meatloaf and all, I still heard dispatch. Clear as a bell. They were still calling out my operator number, and I still knew that the routing system was tracking my movements. The mood was better, but the tracking and the voices... they didn't stop. Not at all. It makes you wonder how the system works, you know? If it's satellite-based or if it's using the local cell towers. They say 5G can pinpoint you within a few feet. I read an article about that in Popular Mechanics. I've had a subscription to Popular Mechanics since 1985. Great magazine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I read an article about that in Popular Mechanics. I've had a subscription to Popular Mechanics since 1985. Great magazine.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave the voices and beliefs been present continuously for the past year and eight months, without a symptom-free period?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah, it's been about that long. I'd say a year and eight months, pretty continuously. The voices, the beliefs about the scanners... they haven't stopped once. It's like a machine that just won't turn off. Speaking of machines, I used to work on this old Massey Ferguson tractor back in the day. Beautiful piece of equipment. I remember when we used to just use a simple color-coded tag system. Red for outbound, blue for receiving, yellow for hazardous materials. Simple, right? You could look across the dock and know exactly what was where. Now you gotta scan a barcode that's half torn off, and the scanner beeps at you like you did something wrong. Those scanners, they run on these lithium-ion batteries that barely hold a charge for a full shift. I read an article in Popular Mechanics about how those batteries are terrible for the environment. They mine the stuff over in South America, I think. Huge open-pit mines. It's a shame what we're doing to the planet just so we can have our gadgets. I try to do my part, you know? I recycle all my aluminum cans. Been doing it for years. I crush them down with this heavy-duty can crusher I mounted on the wall in the garage right next to the pegboard I was telling you about... but yeah, my memory is sharp as a tack. No problems there.\n\nDoctor: Since the voices and scanner messages began, how has your ability to do your job changed?\n\nPatient: Well, after that whole incident with the forklift, they actually took me off operating duties. Put me on leave. They told me I wasn't safe around the equipment anymore. Which is ridiculous, I've been driving those things for thirty years. I know every inch of a Crown reach truck. Did you know they manufacture those right here in the US? Over in Ohio, I think. Same place my grandfather had his farm. Anyway, they said I was a liability. So now I'm just at home. And honestly, my wife has had to take over basically everything. The bills, making appointments, most of the chores around the house. I just can't seem to keep up with it all reliably anymore. She's got this whole system now with a binder and color-coded tabs for the bills. Reminds me of the old color-coded tags we used to have at the warehouse before they brought in those scanners. She uses those little sticky flags, the ones that you can write on? I tried to buy her some more at the office supply store, but they were completely out of the neon green ones. She only likes the neon green for the electric bill. I don't know why, maybe because electricity is energy and green means go? Or maybe it's just her favorite color. Her sister's favorite color is purple, and she drives this awful purple PT Cruiser. Have you ever seen one of those? They look like little hearses. I don't know what Chrysler was thinking when they designed that car. It's got no horsepower, either. But yeah, I'm on leave and she's doing all the heavy lifting at home.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the warehouse system and its messages, have you had any other unusual experiences or beliefs that people around you do not share?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just the scanner screens, if that's what you mean. The voices... they don't stop when I leave the warehouse. I can be sitting right there in my garage, you know, out by the pegboard where I keep all my tools. I like to keep everything organized by size. Wrenches on the left, screwdrivers on the right. Phillips head, flathead, Torx... you really need a Torx set these days if you want to fix anything electronic. They use those special star-shaped screws just to keep you from opening things up and fixing them yourself. It's a racket, really. Planned obsolescence, they call it. They want you to just throw it away and buy a new one. Like those toaster ovens. I had one from the 80s that lasted twenty years. The new one I bought? The heating element went out in six months. Six months! And when I tried to take the back off, sure enough, Torx screws. But anyway, I'll be sitting out there in the garage, everything shut off, quiet as can be, and I'll hear the dispatch voices. Clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous. Saying security is coming for me. It sounds just like the PA system at work, but I'm miles away. My wife says it's just the wind or the neighbor's TV, but I know what I hear. Our neighbor, Frank, he does watch his TV way too loud. He's hard of hearing, refuses to get hearing aids. Says they cost too much. Which they do, honestly. The markup on medical devices is criminal. But Frank's TV doesn't sound like dispatch. Dispatch has this metallic echo to it. And they're definitely talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you lost interest in activities or relationships that used to matter to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... I guess I have. I used to love going fishing. Every Sunday morning, out at the lake before the sun even came up. The water is so still then, you know? It's like glass. And my choir practice, too. I've been singing bass in the church choir for fifteen years. We did this beautiful rendition of 'How Great Thou Art' last Easter. The acoustics in our church are fantastic, they built it back in the 50s with real oak paneling. Not like the cheap drywall they use everywhere now. You can really hear the resonance when the whole choir hits a chord. But lately... I don't know. I just don't go anymore. Same with my garage. I used to spend hours out there, tinkering with wood, building birdhouses, fixing things. I got this beautiful set of chisels for my birthday a few years ago. High-carbon steel, made in Sheffield, England. They hold an edge like you wouldn't believe. You have to sharpen them with a wet stone, though, none of that electric grinder nonsense or you'll ruin the temper of the metal. I keep them right there on the pegboard. But now... I can go out to the garage, sit there, look right at my rods, look at my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them. No desire to make anything. I just sit there. And then I hear the dispatch voices, like I said. So yeah, I guess I don't care about those things much right now.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices and beliefs continued during periods when you were not feeling depressed or unusually energized?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, they do. Actually, there was a stretch a little while back... my mood had eased up some, you know? I wasn't feeling so down in the dumps. I was even eating a bit more, talking a little more with my wife. We had meatloaf one night. She makes a really good meatloaf, puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It caramelizes in the oven, gives it this nice crust. You have to use a mix of beef and pork, though, or it gets too dry. Some people use veal, but I think veal is just too expensive these days. Have you seen the price of meat at the grocery store? It's highway robbery. Ground chuck is up to five dollars a pound. I remember when it was ninety-nine cents. You could feed a whole family on five bucks back then. But anyway, even during those weeks when I was feeling a bit better, eating the meatloaf and all, I still heard dispatch. Clear as a bell. They were still calling out my operator number, and I still knew that the routing system was tracking my movements. The mood was better, but the tracking and the voices... they didn't stop. Not at all. It makes you wonder how the system works, you know? If it's satellite-based or if it's using the local cell towers. They say 5G can pinpoint you within a few feet. I read an article about that in Popular Mechanics. I've had a subscription to Popular Mechanics since 1985. Great magazine.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices and beliefs been present continuously for the past year and eight months, without a symptom-free period?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's been about that long. I'd say a year and eight months, pretty continuously. The voices, the beliefs about the scanners... they haven't stopped once. It's like a machine that just won't turn off. Speaking of machines, I used to work on this old Massey Ferguson tractor back in the day. Beautiful piece of equipment. Red paint, solid iron block engine. They don't make them like that anymore. Now everything is computerized, you know? You can't even change the oil on some of these new models without plugging in a diagnostic tool to reset the computer. It's ridiculous. I remember when you could just crawl under there with a crescent wrench and a drain pan. You'd get a little dirty, sure, but it felt like honest work. Now they want you to take it to the dealer and pay them a hundred bucks an hour just to hook it up to a laptop. It's all about control, really. They want to control who can fix things, who can access the information. Just like the warehouse system. But yeah, the voices and the scanner stuff... year and eight months, straight through. No breaks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah, pretty much every day for most of this whole trouble. Even when the warehouse fears were a bit quieter for a stretch, I still felt just... down. Empty. I couldn't get myself back into the fishing, or the woodworking, or even sitting down for a proper family meal. We used to have Sunday dinners, everyone around the table. Roast beef, usually. Sometimes a pork loin if it was on sale. You have to cook pork loin low and slow, or it dries out terribly. My grandmother used to make it with apples and onions, roasted right in the pan. She had this old cast iron skillet, must have weighed ten pounds. Seasoned perfectly, black as pitch. Nothing ever stuck to it. You can't wash cast iron with soap, you know. Just hot water and a stiff brush, then you rub a little oil into it while it's still warm. Keeps it from rusting. People ruin good cast iron all the time putting it in the dishwasher. It's a crime, really. But yeah... I just haven't been able to enjoy any of that. Just empty, nearly every day.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F25.1, F29\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. My buddy Jim did drafting. He moved out to Arizona a few years ago. Says the dry air is better for his joints. My knees have been killing me lately, honestly. I really need to get that truck looked at, it's been stalling out at red lights...\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed that the warehouse routing system is sending messages specifically to you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on for a while now. The voices and that whole thing with the scanners, I'd say it's been about a year and eight months. Right around the time they brought in those new supervisor kids. You know, they hire these kids straight out of college, business degrees and all that, and they think they know everything about moving freight. One of them, I think his name was Kyle or maybe Kevin, he wore these bright red sneakers on the warehouse floor. Can you believe that? Red sneakers. No steel toes, no nothing. OSHA would have a field day with that. I remember when safety actually meant something. Back in '92, we had a guy drop a pallet of engine parts on his foot because he wasn't paying attention. Broke three toes. He was out for six weeks, and when he came back, he had this special boot he had to wear. It looked like a ski boot. I used to ski, actually, up in Vermont when I was younger. My brother had a cabin up there. We'd go up for the weekend, freeze our tails off, but it was beautiful. The snow up there is different, you know? It's like powder. Not like the slush we get down here. But yeah, about a year and eight months since the system started acting up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear the voices when you are away from the warehouse and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't stop when I clock out. Even when I'm just sitting out in my garage on a Saturday, you know, with the radio off, everything shut down... I hear the dispatch voices. Clear as a bell. They call out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous or that security is on their way to get me. I spend a lot of time in the garage these days. I've been trying to organize my tools, but it's tough. I bought this pegboard from the hardware store, the one over on 4th Street. Used to be a family-owned place, but a big chain bought them out last year. You can't find anyone to help you in there anymore. I walked up and down the aisles for twenty minutes just looking for some specific masonry drill bits. I was going to hang a shelf for my wife's gardening stuff. She loves her tomatoes, gets those heirloom seeds from a catalog... but yeah, the voices, they find me even in the garage.\n\nDoctor: Did anything medically significant happen around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: No, I... I can't think of anything medical happening back then. I mean, my health's been about the same as it always is. Just the usual aches and pains, you know? Like I said about my knees. And my lower back, that acts up if I sit in the cab too long without stretching. I used to have this chiropractor, Dr. Evans. Great guy, had these strong hands, could crack your back and make you feel like a new man. He had this office down by the old strip mall, the one they tore down to build that new luxury apartment complex. Can you believe the rent they're charging for those places? Three thousand a month for a one-bedroom. Who can afford that? Mostly those tech kids, I guess. The ones who work from home and get their groceries delivered. I still like going to the store myself. You gotta pick your own produce, you know? You can't trust someone else to pick a good melon. You gotta thump it, smell the rind. My grandfather taught me that. He had a farm out in Ohio... but no, no big medical stuff around the time the scanners started acting up. Just the usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any new problems with your memory or thinking since the voices and scanner messages began?\n\nPatient: No, my memory is fine. I haven't noticed any issues with my thinking or anything like that. I mean, I still remember all the routing codes for the warehouse, even the old ones they phased out back in 2015. They switched to this new alphanumeric system, supposedly to make things more efficient, but it just confused half the guys on the floor. I remember when we used to just use a simple color-coded tag system. Red for outbound, blue for receiving, yellow for hazardous materials. Simple, right? You could look across the dock and know exactly what was where. Now you gotta scan a barcode that's half torn off, and the scanner beeps at you like you did something wrong. Those scanners, they run on these lithium-ion batteries that barely hold a charge for a full shift. I read an article in Popular Mechanics about how those batteries are terrible for the environment. They mine the stuff over in South America, I think. Huge open-pit mines. It's a shame what we're doing to the planet just so we can have our gadgets. I try to do my part, you know? I recycle all my aluminum cans. Been doing it for years. I crush them down with this heavy-duty can crusher I mounted on the wall in the garage right next to the pegboard I was telling you about... but yeah, my memory is sharp as a tack. No problems there.\n\nDoctor: Since the voices and scanner messages began, how has your ability to do your job changed?\n\nPatient: Well, after that whole incident with the forklift, they actually took me off operating duties. Put me on leave. They told me I wasn't safe around the equipment anymore. Which is ridiculous, I've been driving those things for thirty years. I know every inch of a Crown reach truck. Did you know they manufacture those right here in the US? Over in Ohio, I think. Same place my grandfather had his farm. Anyway, they said I was a liability. So now I'm just at home. And honestly, my wife has had to take over basically everything. The bills, making appointments, most of the chores around the house. I just can't seem to keep up with it all reliably anymore. She's got this whole system now with a binder and color-coded tabs for the bills. Reminds me of the old color-coded tags we used to have at the warehouse before they brought in those scanners. She uses those little sticky flags, the ones that you can write on? I tried to buy her some more at the office supply store, but they were completely out of the neon green ones. She only likes the neon green for the electric bill. I don't know why, maybe because electricity is energy and green means go? Or maybe it's just her favorite color. Her sister's favorite color is purple, and she drives this awful purple PT Cruiser. Have you ever seen one of those? They look like little hearses. I don't know what Chrysler was thinking when they designed that car. It's got no horsepower, either. But yeah, I'm on leave and she's doing all the heavy lifting at home.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the warehouse system and its messages, have you had any other unusual experiences or beliefs that people around you do not share?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just the scanner screens, if that's what you mean. The voices... they don't stop when I leave the warehouse. I can be sitting right there in my garage, you know, out by the pegboard where I keep all my tools. I like to keep everything organized by size. Wrenches on the left, screwdrivers on the right. Phillips head, flathead, Torx... you really need a Torx set these days if you want to fix anything electronic. They use those special star-shaped screws just to keep you from opening things up and fixing them yourself. It's a racket, really. Planned obsolescence, they call it. They want you to just throw it away and buy a new one. Like those toaster ovens. I had one from the 80s that lasted twenty years. The new one I bought? The heating element went out in six months. Six months! And when I tried to take the back off, sure enough, Torx screws. But anyway, I'll be sitting out there in the garage, everything shut off, quiet as can be, and I'll hear the dispatch voices. Clear as day. They're calling out my operator number, telling me I'm dangerous. Saying security is coming for me. It sounds just like the PA system at work, but I'm miles away. My wife says it's just the wind or the neighbor's TV, but I know what I hear. Our neighbor, Frank, he does watch his TV way too loud. He's hard of hearing, refuses to get hearing aids. Says they cost too much. Which they do, honestly. The markup on medical devices is criminal. But Frank's TV doesn't sound like dispatch. Dispatch has this metallic echo to it. And they're definitely talking to me.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, have you lost interest in activities or relationships that used to matter to you?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... I guess I have. I used to love going fishing. Every Sunday morning, out at the lake before the sun even came up. The water is so still then, you know? It's like glass. And my choir practice, too. I've been singing bass in the church choir for fifteen years. We did this beautiful rendition of 'How Great Thou Art' last Easter. The acoustics in our church are fantastic, they built it back in the 50s with real oak paneling. Not like the cheap drywall they use everywhere now. You can really hear the resonance when the whole choir hits a chord. But lately... I don't know. I just don't go anymore. Same with my garage. I used to spend hours out there, tinkering with wood, building birdhouses, fixing things. I got this beautiful set of chisels for my birthday a few years ago. High-carbon steel, made in Sheffield, England. They hold an edge like you wouldn't believe. You have to sharpen them with a wet stone, though, none of that electric grinder nonsense or you'll ruin the temper of the metal. I keep them right there on the pegboard. But now... I can go out to the garage, sit there, look right at my rods, look at my tools, and I just feel... nothing. No pull to touch them. No desire to make anything. I just sit there. And then I hear the dispatch voices, like I said. So yeah, I guess I don't care about those things much right now.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices and beliefs continued during periods when you were not feeling depressed or unusually energized?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, they do. Actually, there was a stretch a little while back... my mood had eased up some, you know? I wasn't feeling so down in the dumps. I was even eating a bit more, talking a little more with my wife. We had meatloaf one night. She makes a really good meatloaf, puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It caramelizes in the oven, gives it this nice crust. You have to use a mix of beef and pork, though, or it gets too dry. Some people use veal, but I think veal is just too expensive these days. Have you seen the price of meat at the grocery store? It's highway robbery. Ground chuck is up to five dollars a pound. I remember when it was ninety-nine cents. You could feed a whole family on five bucks back then. But anyway, even during those weeks when I was feeling a bit better, eating the meatloaf and all, I still heard dispatch. Clear as a bell. They were still calling out my operator number, and I still knew that the routing system was tracking my movements. The mood was better, but the tracking and the voices... they didn't stop. Not at all. It makes you wonder how the system works, you know? If it's satellite-based or if it's using the local cell towers. They say 5G can pinpoint you within a few feet. I read an article about that in Popular Mechanics. I've had a subscription to Popular Mechanics since 1985. Great magazine.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices and beliefs been present continuously for the past year and eight months, without a symptom-free period?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's been about that long. I'd say a year and eight months, pretty continuously. The voices, the beliefs about the scanners... they haven't stopped once. It's like a machine that just won't turn off. Speaking of machines, I used to work on this old Massey Ferguson tractor back in the day. Beautiful piece of equipment. Red paint, solid iron block engine. They don't make them like that anymore. Now everything is computerized, you know? You can't even change the oil on some of these new models without plugging in a diagnostic tool to reset the computer. It's ridiculous. I remember when you could just crawl under there with a crescent wrench and a drain pan. You'd get a little dirty, sure, but it felt like honest work. Now they want you to take it to the dealer and pay them a hundred bucks an hour just to hook it up to a laptop. It's all about control, really. They want to control who can fix things, who can access the information. Just like the warehouse system. But yeah, the voices and the scanner stuff... year and eight months, straight through. No breaks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year and eight months, have you felt depressed nearly every day for most of that time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much every day for most of this whole trouble. Even when the warehouse fears were a bit quieter for a stretch, I still felt just... down. Empty. I couldn't get myself back into the fishing, or the woodworking, or even sitting down for a proper family meal. We used to have Sunday dinners, everyone around the table. Roast beef, usually. Sometimes a pork loin if it was on sale. You have to cook pork loin low and slow, or it dries out terribly. My grandmother used to make it with apples and onions, roasted right in the pan. She had this old cast iron skillet, must have weighed ten pounds. Seasoned perfectly, black as pitch. Nothing ever stuck to it. You can't wash cast iron with soap, you know. Just hot water and a stiff brush, then you rub a little oil into it while it's still warm. Keeps it from rusting. People ruin good cast iron all the time putting it in the dishwasher. It's a crime, really. But yeah... I just haven't been able to enjoy any of that. Just empty, nearly every day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
